the passage and the stairs to see that no one was listening or watching, then locked yourself in the back chapel room, and prostituted yourself for hours together. You have dishonoured the names of your father and husband, and have the effrontery to boast of it. Well might the gardeners, when they used to pass your dwelling, say, "This is the house of that b—old wh—M'Farlane, who used to live in the gardens; she keeps a chapel and a brothel." I was so infatuated, I would not believe any of those reports, but am now fully convinced; indeed your obscenities with me, and your repeated pollutions of my person ought to have opened my eyes, but under a spell, I mistook your profligacy for affection. All you wanted was to satisfy your obscene propensities, and enjoy yourself at my expense, and when you found all the money gone, like other prostitutes, you laugh at me for a fool, and insult me in the most disgusting language, fit only for a Westminster wh; but you shall not go unpunished. I write by this very post to Mrs. Talk, and others of your family, and disclose the whole of your profligate conduct. You—to dare to say that you have done anything for my family; they never had an ounce of bread from you but what you charged them the price of a loaf. You have shamefully robbed them and me. You directed your thief of a son to enter in the book double the amount of whatever they had. You have lived upon my money for the last four months, and you never rendered an account. Where did you get the money to pay for your son's shoes, but in my purse? Did you not charge me for Sophia's expenses down to Derby, when you had more than the amount sent you by Miss Graham and Mrs. Woolf? Did you not have the meanness to charge the 3 s. 6 d., which your paramour, Meredith, deducted from the linen-draper's bill? when you had so much money of mine in your hands, you would not even allow poor Augusta a penny for medicine, while you was launching me into all sorts of extravagance, in supplying you with gin, &c., &c. Instead of a virtuous woman, I found in you a cursing, swearing, blaspheming, gin-drinking prostitute, who will go with the lowest fellow, provided he can treat you—a woman of the most vulgar and dirty, lazy habits—who blushes at nothing—call to her son, in the presence of my daughter, to fetch the p—p—, and squat and ease herself before him—and O, horrid! allows that son, seventeen years old, to sleep with her, when she is aware of his having obscene books and pictures in his possession. Infamous woman, these are the actions for which you ought to be horse-whipped out of Battersea; and wherever you go, there ought to be a post and a lantern with the words, 'Beware of a prostitute.' Simpson had often told me that you were a bad lot altogether, and that you were one of the most artful prostitutes in Battersea, but I was fool enough not to listen to him. Monster, you have obtained your wishes in bringing me to my grave—you are my murderess. I shall haunt you during your life, and when I meet you in hell, will torment you to eternity—you will wither and die a miserable old wretch. Miserable man that I am, to have placed my affections on such a prostitute and monster; but my children will revenge me I hope, and make you restore all you have robbed them of, and give an account of my money. You have lived for years by your prostitution, and obtaining possession of the unfortunate property. Well might you, with your two days' work, wear nothing but silk stockings and French shoes, and lull yourself in bed till nine o'clock, while my poor unfortunate children were obliged to do your dirty work. You and that old b—d, Rock, dare to call my daughter a wh—; look how she works, and that will be at once a complete denial of all you artfully tried to insinuate in my mind against her; but she has found friends, who will punish you for what you have done to her. I have informed her of all your
obscene actions with me—your debauchery and profligacy; and you may rest assured she will revenge me. I send this letter unsealed, on purpose that it may be read at the post-office, and that all Battersea may know what a profligate wh—you are. The letter to Mrs. Talk is also left unsealed for the same purpose; independent of which, I left a paper on my table which shall appear in the papers, that you may not have a chance of escaping the opprobrium and the scorn you so richly deserve, and prevent other miserable beings from falling into your snares. Go, wretched wh—, and carry on your trade fairly at Wapping, amongst your companions, and don't assume again the cloak of religion to hide your profligate conduct. Complete the education of your worthy son—he cannot have a better tutoress in the art of iniquity; you can make him fit for anything, and prepare his way to the gallows. Love, jealousy, and despair—all your works, have brought me to complete madness—my pen curses you—yet, in my dying moments, my poor heart still loves you. Farewell for ever.— A. DALMAS. "
B—"To Mrs. Talk, near the Bridge, Battersea, Surrey. Madam,—That consummate prostitute, M'Farlane, is a dishonour to the name and to your family, and it is high time you should be acquainted with her profligate conduct. She has carried on an illicit intercourse for some years with a married man—that profligate Meredith, the nephew of Mr. Breeze, the linendraper, at Kensington. With all her cunning she could not prevent my children from discovering her licentious libertinism; and yet this is the woman that I unfortunately did love—fatal is the hour I ever admitted her in my house; but under the mask of hypocrisy she resolved my ruin. She had hardly closed the eyes of my dying wife, than, by the most lascivious caresses, she created the fatal feelings which have now brought me to perdition. She made a secret entrance from hers into my house, that she might have access at any hour, without being perceived by her sister Ellen. Alarmed at the progress of the fatal passion she was to artfully raising in my bosom, I resolved to fly from the scene, and accordingly abandoned my home, family, and business, with the full intention of destroying myself; but the recollection of a faithful and affectionate daughter, who was hastening home from America, prevented me; she met me at Liverpool, and persuaded me to come back to London, where, on my arrival, that Millwood, M'Farlane, came to meet me. She invited me to her house, where she renewed her lascivious caresses, professed the most ardent affection for me, saying that since the death of her husband she had never loved any one like me; she readily accepted me as her future husband, and induced me to publish the banns in Kensington church, and our marriage was actually fixed for the 16th of Jan. last. Meanwhile she had received a large sum of money for me, which she wanted me to give to her son, to set him up in some business; however, having received some hints that she was carrying on an illicit intercourse with that profligate, Meredith, I hesitated; she then made up her mind to rob me of part of the money, and make me spend the remainder in debauchery with her, play-going three times per week, suppers, gin-drinking, coaches, cabs, omnibuses; nothing stood in the way. She then began to amuse herself, as she calls it, in exciting the passions to an unnatural degree by acts of the grossest indecencies, and repeated pollutions of my person, exposing her bosom for me to suck her breast, and all sorts of obscenities that can be practised in the brothel; stopping with me till one and two o'clock in the morning; had me at her house on the Sundays, and while prayers were offering to God below, was amusing herself in her libertinism with me, sending word down to Mr. Baker that she was very poorly. You now know the cause of her absence from chapel. Ask at the toll-gate at what
hour she used to come home in the morning; interrogate my children, for even their presence did not stop her from her indecencies. Finding my money going fast, I wanted once more to fly from such a den of infamy, and, under pretence of having some business in Paris, asked her to let me have 10 l. of my money: but no, that was not her game; she would not see me depart till she had quite done with me. Alarmed at my awful situation, I began to make repeated inquiries, and found all my suspicions well founded, that every one of the Battersea gardeners knew her to be a common prostitute. I taxed her with her illicit intercourse with Meredith, and she had the unblushing effrontery that she should still carry it on; and to prevent her sister from observing when he comes, she meets him at that old bawd's, Mrs. Rock. What will you think when I inform you of her allowing her son, seventeen years old, to sleep with her, when she admitted to me herself that he had in his possession obscene books and pictures; and when she comes home, even in the presence of my youngest daughter, calls out to Bill to bring her the po, and squats herself before him. Horrid! Will you after this look upon that infamous woman as your sister? Impossible. Having now given up the chapel, she intends to turn her house into a brothel, by taking none but single men lodgers. What a miserable and unfortunate man I have been to put my affections in the power of such a vulgar prostitute. Now that she has completed my ruin, she most grossly insults me, and even to my daughter calls me Mr. Kiss my * * *. Could I have expected more disgusting language from a Westminster wh—? Heartless wretch! When, last Sunday week, she kept me in debauchery till four o'clock in the morning at her house, I informed her that my mind was in that state that I should cut my throat; she very coolly said, "Don't do it here, it will make such a mess." Can you any longer allow that infamous prostitute to dishonour your maiden name, and allow her to call you sister? She has driven me mad—mad—mad; she has murdered me! A. DALMAS. "
C—"To my unfortunate family—Be it known, that infernal prostitute, M'Farlane, of No. 13, Bridge-road, Battersea, that second Millwood, has brought me to perdition; she has robbed me and my family under the mask of affection; she has led me into all sorts of debauchery: her profligate and illicit intercourse with that married man, Meredith, the nephew of Breeze, the linen-draper, of Kensington, at the very time she had offered to become my wife, has made me come to the determination of committing suicide. I have been adoring a common prostitute, who now insults me in the most revolting manner. To have been mad enough to put my affection on a swearing, cursing, blaspheming, gin-drinking washerwoman and a wh—, has turned my brain; I can live no longer: let the world know her, and may this prevent other victims from falling into her infernal snares. A. DALMAS. My unfortunate children, my unfortunate family! Farewell Caroline; farewell Charlotte; farewell Sophia; farewell Augusta; farewell for ever: that Millwood M'Farlane has murdered me. A. DALMAS. Avenge my death."
GUILTY . Aged 50.— DEATH .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440610-1776]
1776. WILLIAM THATCHER was indicted for embezzlement.
HENRY YATES , watchmaker, Putney. On the 25th of May I paid the prisoner 7 s. on account of Mr. Joyce—I knew him as his servant.
WILLIAM DALE ( policeman.) I took the prisoner in charge—he said, "I have lost the money."
JAMES JOYCE , shoemaker, Wandsworth. The prisoner was in my service
about seventeen months—I sometimes employed him to receive money—he never told me he had received this sum—he was taken in charge on the 28th of May, three days after—I had not sent him to Yates, he went of his own accord—he was not to receive money for me unless I sent him for it—he had no general authority, and ought not to have gone for this—if anybody offered him money he was to leave it—it was a breach of his duty to receive this at all.
HENRY YATES re-examined. A pair of boots had been brought to my house on the Wednesday, and on Saturday the prisoner came to know if they fitted—I said, "Were they paid for?"—he said, "No"—I said, "What were they?"—he said, "7 s.," which I paid him—he did not ask for it.
JAMES JOYCE re-examined. He had no authority to go about these boots at all—he was at liberty to receive the money if offered, but not on my authority—if he had brought it to me I should have taken it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did he not come to you under a written agreement? A. Yes—I received 10 l. with him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440610-1777]
1777. WILLIAM THATCHER was again indicted for embezzlement.
JAMES JOYCE . I sent the prisoner to receive 6 s. 6 d. from Mr. Potter, about the 12th of May—he came home, and said he had not received it—on the 18th of May I sent him to Mrs. Davis for 5 s. 6 d.—he came back, and said she had not paid him, she would call in a day or two.
Cross-examined. Q. What did you say when you sent him? A. I gave him Potter's bill, which was 9 s. 6 d., but it seems he tore off the first item, which was 3 s., and received that first—he has accounted for larger sums on other occasions—he had not to enter payments in any book.
ANN GIBBS . I am servant to Mr. Potter, of Wandsworth Common, who deals with Joyce. I know the prisoner as his servant—I saw White, my fellow-servant, pay him 3 s. 6 d., and on the 23rd of May I paid him 6 s. 6 d. for Mr. Joyce—he wrote "Paid" on each bill, which are here.
ELIZA DAVIS . I am servant to Mr. Colburn. On the 18th of May the prisoner brought me a pair of shoes, for which I paid him 5 s. 6 d. for Mr. Joyce—he wrote "Paid" on the bill.
MR. JOYCE. He never paid me these sums.
Cross-examined. Q. Did his father offer to pay you the money? A. No.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 14.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18440610-1778]
1778. JOHN HIGGINS was indicted for stealing 1 filly, price 20 l., the property of George Rough.
GEORGE ROUGH . I am a market-gardener, and live at Wands worth. On the 14th of May I had a filly, which I saw safe, grazing on the common, at eight o'clock at night, with its mother and others—I missed her next morning, at eight o'clock—did not see her again till the 30th, when she was brought from Salisbury—she was worth 20 l.
EDWD. WENHAM . I am a nurseryman. The prisoner lodged and slept with me at Mrs. Chapman's, Brumhall-road, Clapham, and left about Monday evening, the 12th or 13th of May—we lodged about a mile and three quarters from Mr. Rough's—on Tuesday, Wednesday, or Thursday, the 13th, 14th, or 15th of May, I was at a public-house—the prisoner came in, and asked if I would toss him for half a gallon of ale—I said, "No"—I afterwards tossed him for a pot—he then told me he had got a cob to break in, and was to receive 5 l. for that, he had received 50 s. on account, and asked if I would go
to the races on the Derby day, he would drive me down—I said I should not like to go down on Derby day in the rough, which means with a colt—he showed me a bridle which he had under his waistcoat—we sat there some time—he has not since returned to his lodging—I did not see him again till he was committed from the police court, at Wandsworth.
JAMES FRAMPTON . I live at Elmore-green, near Shaftesbury, which is 103 miles from Wandsworth. On Friday the 17th of May, I met the prisoner at the King's Arms, Shaftesbury—after some conversation he said he had a nice cob filly in the stable for sale—he said, "Step up and look at it"—I went and saw her—he said, "This thing ought to be worth 20 l. here"—I said it might yield 12 l. or 14 l. if a gentleman took a liking to it—it was about thirteen and a half hands high, and three years old—I went to the landlord of the Fountain, and told him a man at the King's Arms had a nice young thing—I met the prisoner next morning coming down to the Fountain with the mare—I returned with him to the Fountain—the landlord looked at the mare and the prisoner at his pony, but they did not deal—it was taken to the King's Arms—Wright gave him his horse, two sovereigns, and a saddle for it.
WILLIAM WRIGHT . On the Saturday I was at Elmore-green, in the parish of Malcombe, looking at some cheeses, and saw the prisoner and the landlord dealing about a young filly—they did not deal—I went and had something to drink, and said, "Cannot you two deal?"—they said, "No"—I said, "I have got a horse at home, I should not mind changing him for it"—he said, "Well, I do not mind changing; I want some money; send for your horse"—I sent for it, and after a bit, gave him my horse, two sovereigns, and a saddle for it—mine was a useful horse—he said he wanted a saddle very bad, he had wrung his young mare's back coming along—he gave me the name of John Batch of Winterbourne at first—here is the paper which he wrote—I asked if he knew anybody in the neighbourhood—he said he knew farmer Burchfield and his sons—I said, "I should like you to go with me, I do not think you have come by it honestly"—he said, "Well, I bought the mare at Newbury fair, and paid for it"—I said, "Go with me to Mr. Burchfield and I shall be satisfied"—we went to Shaftesbury, and saw Mr. Burchfield, who called him by the name of Higgins—I said, "Well, you have given me a wrong name, come back, the horse shall be put into the Crown and Punchbowl"—I gave both horses in charge of the ostler there, and sent for a constable.
Prisoner. He locked the horses up, and asked me to run away—I would not—I was taken and detained nine days, then discharged, but taken again.
WILLIAM JONAS DUNHAM ( constable of Shaftesbury.) On the 18th of May I went to the Crown and Punchbowl, saw the prisoner there, and a brown filly which the prosecutor claimed—the prisoner was remanded till the Monday week, and liberated that day at twelve o'clock—about nine in the evening, two men came from Wandsworth, and claimed the horse, and the prisoner was taken again—he had remained in the town nine hours.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the mare at Newbury fair, on the 16th, for nine guineas—if I had been guilty I should not have stopped in the town nine hours after I was liberated.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440610-1779]
1779. THOMAS JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, at Camberwell, 12 spoons, value 7 l. 9 s.; 7 forks, 3 l. 7 s.; 1 butter knife, 1 l.; and 2 knife rests, 12 s.; the property of Josiah Westley, in his dwelling-house.
HARRIETT JEWELL . I am servant to Josiah Wesley, of Cripplegate-terrace, Camberwell—the plate produced is his property—I saw it safe at twelve
in the morning of the 11th of May, in a basket in the dresser, and missed it soon after—I saw the prisoner going from the area gate, putting something into his pocket—I called out, and followed him nearly three quarters of a mile—he was running—I saw a policeman and described him—he was brought back in about a quarter of an hour—I found the basket by the area door, empty, except two salt spoons.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was nobody at home but you? A. Yes, mistress—I came out of the hall door, and saw the prisoner crossing the grass-plot—the area gate was left open—the house is in the high road—I ran three quarters of a mile without meeting a policeman—I did not know the prisoner before—I had lost sight of him when I met the policeman—he ran into Camberwell-grove—I am quite sure he is the person—I know the plate by constantly using it—the spoons are bruised, and the knife rests loose at the end—there is no initial or crest on them—I have used them for two months.
GEORGE TAYLOR . I am servant to Mr. Curling, of Camberwell. On the 11th of May, about two o'clock, I found this plate under a shrub at master's door, and gave it to the policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. Is your master's in the high road? A. Rather out of it, in the Crescent, Camberwell-grove.
GEORGE TILLSELL ( policeman.) On the 11th of May I was at my bed-room window in the Grove, and saw the prisoner come round the corner, put his hand into his right hand pocket, take out two spoons and two forks, and throw them into my garden—I came down stairs, secured him, took him back to Mr. Westley's—the witness said, "That is the man"—I said, "What has he been doing?"—she said, "He has been robbing us of plate," and claimed what I had picked up—the prisoner came in a direction from Mr. Curling's house—the prosecutor's house is in the parish of Camberwell.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did it take you to get out? A. Not half a minute—I was on the first floor—I saw him directly I got out—he walked a little, but as soon as he saw me he ran—I stopped him about half a mile off, without losing sight of him—I am sure he is the man.
GUILTY . Aged.18— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440610-1781]
1781. CHARLES EDWARDS and WILLIAM JOHNSON were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of May, at St. George the Martyr, 19 yards of woollen cloth, value 17 l., the goods of Samuel Summers, in his dwelling-house; to which
JOHNSON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Ten Years.
SAMUEL SUMMERS . I am a tailor, and live in Bridge-house Place, Newington Causeway, in the parish of St. George. On the 11th of May, Edwards and another man came to my shop—Edwards asked what I would charge him to make a pair of trowsers—I told him the price—he said he had a friend in the drapery business, that he should get the stuff of—the other one, who is not here, said, "Perhaps Mr. Summers can show you some patterns of summer trowsers," which I did—Edwards remarked they were very handsome, and if his friend had not anything to please him better, he would call in the course of the morning and be measured for a pair of them—they then went away—about ten minutes after, the shop bell rang again, and this letter was brought in to me—whilst I was reading it I heard a bustle on the stairs—I ran up, and ran out, bawling "Stop thief"—I ran to the coach-stand at the corner of the Borough-road, and saw the two prisoners in a cab which was standing—I said to Edwards, "What, is it you, sir?"—he said, "Yes, it is me, I know nothing about it," he did not know what I meant, or something
of that sort—Johnson got out of the cab, and tried to get away—a butcher held him till the policeman came—a piece of my cloth was found in the cab, worth 17 l., which had been safe in my shop the evening before—it was not the upper piece—I believe it was under a piece six or seven yards long, which was replaced, and this taken—my shop communicates with the dwelling-house, and forms part of it.
SUSAN MITCHELL . I am the prosecutor's servant. On the 11th of May, about half-past eight o'clock in the morning, I answered a ring at the bell, and saw Edwards—he said he wished to speak with Mr. Summers—I went down into the kitchen, and left Edwards and another man in the shop—master came up to them—between ten minutes and a quarter of an hour afterwards, I answered the shop bell again, and Johnson came into the shop, and gave me this letter to give to my master, saying he would wait for an answer—I carried the letter into the kitchen, leaving Johnson in the shop—whilst I was in the kitchen I heard the door slam—I ran up into the shop—the shop door was about half open, and the prisoner gone—I saw him on the pavement—he had the cloth under his right arm—my master immediately ran after him—the coach-stand is a very little way from our house, it is in sight of the house—a person at the coach-stand could command a view of the shop door.
Edwards. Q. Whereabouts is the coach-stand? A. Just below our house, it is round a corner—I left no one in charge of the shop when I left you here—you had as good an opportunity of stealing the cloth then as after.
RICHARD HARVEY . I am a tailor, and work for Mr. Summers in a back shop, the parlour is between the front and back shops—I can see through the parlour window into the front shop. On the 11th of May I heard the bell ring, and saw Johnson admitted, and saw him deliver a note to the servant—she went in the direction of the kitchen—I saw Johnson pass round the end of the counter towards where the cloths are kept—I kept my eye on the shop, and saw Johnson return with a piece of cloth—Iran through the passage into the front shop—I saw the direction in which he went—he had the cloth under his arm—the cab-stand is about forty yards from the door—part of it is in view of the shop—I should think that a person standing at the nearest extremity of the cab —stand, could command a view of a person going out of the shop into the street—I saw Johnson put the cloth in the cab, and then get in—I and some neighbours, with Mr. Summers, surrounded the cab, and the prisoners were kept there till the constable came—I saw Edwards inside the cab.
Edwards. Q. Did you see me in the shop that morning? A. Yes, I saw a person resembling you, and another—I found the cloth standing endways on the seat of the cab —I did not see it till Johnson had got out and was secured—it was rather more towards where Johnson sat—you might have reached it—the cab was between three and four feet wide, about room for two persons to sit comfortably.
JOB HAYWARD . I am a cab —proprietor in the Borough-road, and have been in the habit of putting on this stand. I was rubbing up my cab —Edwards came behind me, opened the door, got in, and ordered me to St. Martin's-lane—I put him in, and directly went to the horse's head to take the nose-bag off, when Johnson came to the other door, and got into the cab —I saw the side of the cloth—I did not see Johnson come up—I cannot say which way he came—I saw him at the cab —door, getting in—he must have joined us immediately after Edwards had got into the cab, while I was petting to the horse's head—you can see the prosecutor's house perfectly well from the cab —stand—it is about eight or ten doors from it—I was the third cab on the stand—if they had taken the first cab, the man being already on his box, in all probability they might
have got away—the people came up and surrounded the cab-Johnson opened the door, and tried to get away, but Cook came up and caught hold of him.
Edwards. Q. Was there anybody with me? A. Not at first—you ordered me to drive to St. Marlin's-lane—I had to take of the nosebag before we started—I had just got on the dickey when I heard the cry of "Stop"—you gave the order where to drive before Johnson came.
JOHN LLOYD ( Policeman.) I received information and went to the spot—I found Edwards in the corner of the cab sitting quietly, and the piece of cloth in the opposite corner, standing partly upright, and partly down—it looked as if somebody had leaned their back against it—it was rather bent in—on searching him at the station, I found this note, which tallies with the hand-writing of the one Johnson left at Mr. Summers'—it was wafered—I opened it.
( Notes read.)—"Dear Sir,—I should feel obliged if you would let me know when you would call on me to take measure for a jacket for my eldest son Charles. Please to bring some patterns along with you."
"Dear Madam,—I should feel obliged if you would let me know at what rate you would let you drawing-room for a foreigner of distinction.
" HENRY SILVESTER. "
MR. SUMMERS ( re-examined.) This is my cloth—the wafer of the note I received perfectly, and the ink also—I smeared it with my finger.
EDWARDS'S DEFENCE. I think I have proved that the meeting at the cab was purely accidental; I am a foreigner, and unacquainted with the laws of this country; I was found in company with this person, who committed the depredation, but the stolen article remained in his possession, untouched by me; the law may condemn, me but I think I am entitled to your verdict.
EDWARDS — GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18440610-1782]
1782. JOHN LEAVER was indicted for feloniously attempting to administer arsenic to Margaret Leaver, with intent to murder her.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE LEAVER . I am eleven years old. I live with my father and mother, in Bermondsey-st.—the prisoner is my father. On the 29th of may I got the tea things ready for breakfast—my father was down stairs, and my mother up in bed—she was not quite well—it was about nine o'clock—my father was at the table—there were cups—I had placed them in saucer—my father sent me to fetch a penny loaf—I was not long gone—when I returned mother's cup was in the middle was in the table with the saucer over, it which was not so when I went out—my father took it off the cup put the cup in the saucer, and Poured a little drop of tea, in and filled it—he then told me to and go up and ask mother if she would have a cup of tea—I went up, came down, and told him mother said "yes"—he then took the cup, by is side, filled it with tea, put some milk in it, and was skimming the top off the tea, and throwing what he was skimming off on the carpet/—he saw me looking at him, and he took the cup, and put it on the side of the table so that I should not see him, then put some milk in it, stirred it up, and sent it up to mother did with the tea—I came down again—I have seen my father and mother put soda in to the tea-pot—I did not see any put into the tea-pot on this occasion—I know soda when I see it—we have been annoyed by rats, and my father and mother have put arsenic on bread and butter to throw down the rat-holes.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. How do you know soda? A. It
is a white powder—some of it is like allum—what my father and mother use is like powder, it is white—the carpet is in the parlour now—I am still living with my mother—it was a great while ago that father said he would settle mother, several months—they had been quarrelling at the time—my father was kind to me—he has thrashed me—I did not then say I wished he was dead—I never said so in my life—he beat me about a week before this happened, not severely—he beat me on my back with a cane—I did not stay in the room after taking the tea to my mother—she sat it on the wash hand-stand while I was there—I went down—my father sent me up with another cup of tea in another cup—she drank that—I saw my father pour that out—I was not gone above a minute for the loaf—I only had to cross the road—I do not know what mashing tea means—I never saw my mother put tea leaves into a cup and pour hot water on them—they use soda to make it draw—the policeman found a paper of arsenic—I did not tell him where it was—he found it at the back of the till in the shop—I had not seen the paper before—I did not say, "That is it" when it was found—I did not take the policeman to the till, or tell him to go there, nor had my mother that I heard—when I came in with the loaf I went to clean my brother and sister's shoes while my father was preparing my mother's tea—I was in another room just by the parlour door—George, the shopman, was in the shop—my father is a hair-dresser—the shop joins the room where the tea is made—George is not here—I did not tell anybody but my mother what I had seen—I said nothing to my father about it—my father has been a very kind man to me—my mother told me as I was coming in to say nothing but what I saw, and speak the truth—she told me so this morning when I came in here—she has spoken to me about twice about it—I told her about my father hiding the cup that I should not see it, when I took it up to her—it is a round table—my mother often breakfasted in bed when she is ill—my father generally made tea for her—she did not put any to her mouth while I was there—there are five children—I have seen my father give arsenic to the rats for five or six months—I cannot tell the colour of the carpet—it is second-hand and faded, a sort of brown colour—he threw five or six spoonfuls of the stuff on the carpet.
MR. DOANE. Q. How long did you take the second cup up after the first? A. About ten minutes—my father made it, and told me to take it up and fetch the other cup down—I did not bring it down, as mother wanted to save the tea.
MARGARET LEAVER . I have been married to the prisoner eighteen years—we have had eight children, and have five living—we have always been on good terms—we had little differences, as others do—we have not lived so happy lately as formerly—he struck me once, three or four months ago—on the 29th of May I was indisposed, and did not come down to breakfast—my son came to ask if I would have a cup of tea, and afterwards brought me up one—he told me something—I did not take any notice of what he said—it was too hot, and I let it remain—I afterwards drank a small portion of it—it was a great deal too sweet for my palate—I did not swallow it all—I spat some of it out—I put it on the washhand-stand—some time after my son brought me up another cup of tea, which I drank—I did not send the first cup down—I went down about half-past nine, and took the cup down with me—I afterwards went with it to Guy's Hospital, as I looked at it, and saw some bits of white powder in it—I felt a little burning in my throat, but so simple it might be fancy—I showed the powder to the medical gentleman there—I went there to be satisfied privately, what was the powder in the tea—they gave me an emetic—we have rats in the house, and used arsenic to
destroy them ever since we lived there—we kept it in a drawer in-the shop, which had several divisions in it.
Cross-examined. Q. Was that the till? A. Yes, it was kept there—I was present when the policeman found a paper containing arsenic—no one directed him to that drawer—it was not the first place he went to—he came to search the house—there was a quantity of arsenic in it—it looked as if it had not been untied at all—I had a violent fit of coughing in the course of the morning—it was after that I felt the burning in my throat—the coughing might have produced it—my husband is a kind man—he had flogged my son about a week before this—he frequently corrected him when he required it—my son does not remember an injury long, if he did I would flog him equally to his father—he is too forgetful, or he would not so soon transgress again—he was never flogged severely enough to remember it long—I did not swallow more than a teaspoon-full of the tea—my little boy told me that a portion of the contents of the cup were thrown on the carpet—I do not know whether the doctor was told of it.
WILLIAM TIFFEN ILET . I am assistant to Mr. Cooper, surgeon of Guy's Hospital. I remember the prosecutrix bringing a breakfast cup to the Hospital—I poured off the liquid—there was a sediment, which was analysed—it was three or four grains of finely powdered arsenic—in my judgment that would most likely not be sufficient to cause death—one or two grains more would very likely do it—I administered an emetic, but found no arsenic among the contents of the stomach—I have examined the contents of the paper produced—it is arsenic, and is labelled arsenic.
Cross-examined. Q. If I am rightly informed, one thousandth part of a grain, by a proper test, might be discovered? A. Yes—not the slightest vestige was found in the stomach—I was not aware that a portion of the contents of the cup were poured on the carpet—if that had contained arsenic, it might easily have been ascertained—I think the tea poured off from the arsenic contained none.
MR. DOANE. Q. Would the skim have contained arsenic necessarily? A. Not necessarily.
COURT. Q. Would there be a portion of it in a state of solution? A. In the tea there was not the least trace of arsenic—tea dissolves a very small portion of arsenic, indeed—it would sink after being stirred, and become sediment.
DONALD MURRAY ( policeman.) I went to the prisoner's house in Bermondsey-street, and examined, among other places, the drawer where the till is—I found the paper I have produced in the back part of it.
Cross-examined. Q. Who directed you to the drawer? A. The wife.
MR. DOANE. Q. Had you examined other places first? A. Yes—there was a case of drawers in the shop—I commenced at one side, and began to search the whole; and she said she knew it was in the drawer that was locked.
WILLIAM HILL . I am a chemist, and live in Bermondsey-street. I know the prisoner, and have been in the habit of selling him small quantities of arsenic, as well as soda—it is four or five weeks ago since I sold him any—he told me it was to destroy rats—the parcel produced has my label on it—it is about a pennyworth of arsenic.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know him personally? A. I have known him between five and six years—I never heard anything against him—this appears in the state it was when I sold it.
PHILIP M'CORMACK ( policeman.) I took the prisoner, and told him he was charged with attempting to administer poison to his wife—he said he
knew nothing of it, that it was a planned thing between his wife and her companions.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know him? A. I have known him about six years—he bore the character of a respectable man.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440610-1783]

[trialAccount t18440610-1784]
1784. GEORGE FURNESS was indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering a forged request for the delivery of goods, with intent to defraud Joshua Walker and others.
WILLIAM BURNS . I am clerk to Joshua Walker and others, lead-merchants, Belvidere-road, Lambeth. On the 11th of May I received this paper, to the best of my knowledge.
THOMAS WATKINS ( policeman.) I got this paper from Mr. Middleditch.
CHARLES MIDDLEDITCH . I got this paper from the head managing man at Walker's factory, in Burns's presence.
WILLIAM BURNS re-examined. I took it off the file, and gave it to a clerk, who handed it to Middleditch, in my presence—I could not swear that I received that paper from the prisoner—we have such a number of wagons, carts, and orders daily, and such a number of carmen with each team, that I do not know who gave it me—Mr. Middleditch is a customer of our house—I have no recollection of putting that particular order on the file—I saw a cart in the yard, to the best of my recollection—as soon as I receive an order, I give a verbal order to the warehouseman to deliver the goods to the parties.
THOMAS SHILLAM . I am warehouseman in the white-lead department at Messrs. Walker's. On the 11th of May, I received a verbal order from Burns to pack two firkins of lead—I packed it—and noticed a cart backed up to the warehouse gates—the prisoner had charge of it—I asked him whether he came for pipe or paint—he said for paint—white lead means paint in the trade—I asked him who it was for—he said for Mr. Middleditch—I saw the two firkins craned into the cart—I weighed them, and gave the weight out on the slate—I am sure the prisoner is the person—there was nobody else with the cart—he received the goods.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You had never seen him before? A. No, nor since, till he was in custody—I was positive he was the man—but I must confess that yesterday morning while looking at the list on the gates in the yard outside, I saw a person very much resembling him—and I was so much struck with the resemblance that I mentioned it to Mr. Middleditch and Burns—I was certain of the prisoner before that, but having seen that person my belief is very much shaken—I do not think that man was more like the person that came than the prisoner, but so strongly resembling him—that man was not apprehended—I had never seen him before.
COURT. Q. Did you trace the cart at all? A. No—the name of "C. Middleditch" was painted on it in white letters, not in the ordinary way, but on a flat piece of wood, as if put on temporarily—I do not know whether the man I saw here yesterday was any relation of the prisoner's—if he had been apprehended instead of the prisoner I firmly believe I should have sworn to him—after that I do entertain a doubt whether the prisoner is the man.
CHARLES MIDDLEDITCH. My cart was out on the 11th of May, in charge
of Wilson, my carman—he is not here—my name was painted on the pannel, between the beading.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18440610-1785]
1785. GEORGE CLARK , ANDREW MURPHY , CHARLES SAUNDERS , and EMMA FARMER , were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Robert Taylor, at St. Mary, Newington, about 2 o'clock in the night of the 10th of May, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 2 bottles, value 1 l.5 s.; 1 neck-chain, 8 s.; 1 cushion, 6 d.; 17 tin checks, 4 d.; 14 groats, 47 pence, 169 halfpence, and 6 farthings, his property: 2nd COUNT, for burglariously breaking out of the said dwelling-house; and that Clark and Farmer had been before convicted of felony.
ROBERT TAYLOR . I keep the Black-horse, Kent-street, St. Mary, Newington. On the night of the 10th of May I went to bed about five minutes or a quarter to one—I left the house fast—I cannot swear that the window was fast, but the other part was—the policeman called me about four o'clock—I came down and found parties had got in at the back window of the club-room, come down, opened the bar door, unlocked it, and gone out the back way—the articles stated were taken—there was 13 s. in copper, 5 s. in silver, a gilt chain, an emery cushion, and 17 tin tickets or checks with my initials "R. T." on them—the window was on one pair of stairs, it looks into a yard—I know the prisoner Saunders—he has used my house for the last four or five months.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. You do not know whether the window was open or shut? A. I know it was shut, but I do not know whether it was fastened—the catch will not fasten—I went and looked at it that night.
BENJAMIN PARSONS ( police-constable M. 107.) On the morning of Saturday, the 11th of May, about aquarter-past two, I was on duty in Kent-street, and saw the prisoner Farmer in Black-horse-court, against Mr. Taylor's premises—directly she saw me, she turned back and gave Clark a signal—I could not understand what it was—I was not near enough—I went up to Clark, and saw Murphy come over the wall of Mr. Taylor's premises, on to Clark's shoulder in Black-horse-court, close against Farmer—I took Clark into custody—Murphy made his escape into the Dover-road—I took Clark with me, and saw a brother constable in the Dover-road—I sung out, "Stop him," and he immediately laid hold of Murphy—we took them to the station—on Clark I found 8 s. 6 d. in copper, fourteen check tickets, and one emery cushion—which I produce—Farmer followed us to the station-house—when we got there we took her—she was searched, and nothing found on her.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long was it previous to seeing Murphy coming down out of the yard that you saw Farmer? A. Just before she went and gave Clark the signal—it was a signal they have got among themselves—I was not near enough to tell what it was—I heard her say something, but could not understand what it was exactly—I could not understand whether it was "a crush " or" a bobby "—I cannot swear that I heard any words of that kind—I heard her say something—it was a signal to let them know a policeman was coming—I will undertake to swear Farmer had seen me—she was meeting me just as I was against the corner of the court—after she turned back from me she made the signal—she was nine or ten yards off, not half a stone's throw from the other prisoners—she was close against Clark, close enough for him to hear what she said—she was very near the top of the court when she saw me, and then she returned and gave Clark the signal.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Black-horse-court is a public thoroughfare, is it not? A. Yes—one end is in Dover-road and the other in Kent-street—there five or six houses in the court—I do not know the exact length of it.
EDWARD SPICE ( police-constable M 180.) On Saturday morning, the 11th of May, about half-past one o'clock, I was on duty in the Dover-road, and observed the prisoners, Farmer and Saunders, in Black Horse-court—when I returned, about a quarter-past two, I saw them in the court again—I watched them, and as soon as they saw me coming the second time, they walked through into Kent-street—I returned again about two, and they were still remaining in the court, watching the wall belonging to Mr. Taylor's premises—when they saw me again they walked away—about ten minutes past two I was watching them—Saunders and Farmer were both looking at the wall, as if looking for some person, and at that time the sergeant came up Kent-street, and drove them from there into the Dover-road—he came the opposite way from me—they came into Lawson-street, up Brand-street, and there Saunders left Farmer—she came out into the Dover-road—I stopped her, and said if I saw her in the court again I should take her to the station—she said she was going home—she went directly down Black Horse-court—I followed her very closely, and just before I got to the court I saw Murphy run out of the court into the Dover-road—at that time Parsons saw me, and said, "Is that you?"—I said, "Yes"—he gave me an alarm, and I took Murphy into custody—I found on him 2 s. 7 d. in copper, five 4 d. pieces, some lucifers, two knives, a handkerchief, and three tin tickets with "RT" on them.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. The last time you saw Farmer, previous to the arrest of these parties, was in the Dover-road? A. Yes—she left me there, and came directly back to the Black Horse again.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Yow saw them four separate times? A. Yes—Farmer and Saunders were talking together in the court—I did not see them talking at the end of the court—the first time I saw them watching was at half-past one o'clock—two o'clock was the second time—I said so before the Magistrate—I do not know that all I said was taken down—I stated just the same here as I did before the Magistrate—this is my name and hand-writing—( the deposition being read, did not state that he saw them watching)—Saunders had got away—I did not take him at his own lodging—I found him lodging at Falcon-court, in the Borough.
MR. TAYLOR re-examined. These tickets marked "R. T." are mine, and are what were taken out of my house—this emery cushion is Mrs. Taylor's, and was taken out of her dressing-case.
Murphy's Defence. I never saw Saunders till in Horsemonger-lane; I have seen Farmer along with Clark, but not for a week before that night; I can assure you Saunders is as innocent as a child unborn.
THOMAS WILLIAM REDFORD ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of Clark's former conviction—( read)—he is the same person.
JUSTIN M'CARTHY ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of Farmer's former conviction—( read)—she is the person.
CLARK — GUILTY . Aged 27.
MURPHY — GUILTY . Aged 22.
SAUNDERS — GUILTY . Aged 27.
FARMER — GUILTY . Aged 32.
Transported for twenty years.
Before Mr. Recorder.

[trialAccount t18440610-1787]
1787. PHILIP CHILD was indicted for stealing 4 sovereigns, 8 half-sovereigns, 3 half-crowns, 12 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Charleslon.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES ION . I am a milkman, and live in John-street, Vassal-road, Brixton. The prisoner married my sister—he was out of work some time ago, and I allowed him, his wife, and children to remain in my house for about six weeks or two months, without paying any rent until he should be enabled to get work—I used to keep my money in a little box, locked up in a chest under our bed—I saw it about nine o'clock in the evening of the 14th, and there was then 8 l. in sovereigns and half-sovereigns, and 1 l. in silver—I had put it there to pay my rent with—on the 16th, I went out about half-past four to my work—I came home about eight to breakfast, about twelve to dinner, and when I came home at night the prisoner was gone—he had given me no intimation of his leaving—he did not come home that night—I suspected something might be wrong, and my wife missed the money—I directly went to the station, and gave information of the robbery—on the 2nd of May, I received a letter of which I took no notice—a friend came to me last Saturday week, and in consequence of what he said, I met the prisoner that evening at Kennington church, and gave him into custody—the person who came to me was with him.
Cross-examined by MB. PAYNE. Q. What is that person's name? A. I do not know—I took a policeman with me—I never made any effort to find the prisoner—he has gone away when out of work on former occasions—I cannot say where to—he might have gone into the country in search of work—I have got a trifling subscription among my friends to repay the money—I had no conversation with the prisoner when I met him—I gave him into custody directly—I had made no arrangement with the person who came to me—I merely agreed to go to the place.
MARY ION . I am the prosecutor's wife. I saw the money safe on the 15th of Jan., at two o'clock, when I went to the box and took out 2 s.—I left the 8 l. in gold, and 1 l. in silver—locked the box up safe, and put it under the bed down stairs—I went out, leaving the prisoner and his wife in the house—I returned at half-past five in the evening, and he was gone—his wife and children were there—I did not see him again till he was in custody—next day I went to look for my money, and it was gone—the box was locked, and in the same place I had left it—the key was in my pocket—a false key must have been used—it is a common lock.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite sure the prisoner was not at home that night? A. I am positive—I went into the room at half-past five o'clock when I came home, and many times after, but did not see him—their room was next to mine in which the money was—there were lodgers in the house, but they did not come home till between nine and ten—they do not have to pass our room—it is not far from the stairs.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did either of the lodgers run away next day? A.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440610-1788]
1788. CHARLES WARD and FREDERICK WARD were indicted for stealing 36 yards of lace edging, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Robert Parritt; and that Charles Ward had been before convicted of felony.
ROBERT PARRITT , haberdasher, Great Suffolk-street, Southwark. WILLIAM MATREY . I live with my father in Suffolk-street. On the 1st of May, I saw the two prisoners together at the prosecutor's window about seven o'clock—I saw them take the lace out of the window and go away with it under their jackets—they came back in about a quarter of an hour, and were then taken—I am sure they are the same persons—I had seen them about before, and knew them.
JURY. Q. Which of them had hold of the lace? A. Frederick, but they were both together.
JOHN DELANEY ( police-constable M 100.) I took the prisoners, but found nothing on them.
Charles Ward. I did not take it.
Frederick Ward. I was passing, and two boys were looking very hard in the window, the man came out and took us.

This ring was in my room; it belongs to my daughter's husband, Moses Marks, and this watch, guard, and eight sovereigns were safe in a cupboard in my bed-room on the evening of Sunday week—I missed them on Monday morning, and the prisoner too—she was my servant—I did not see her again till Tuesday morning, when her mother fetched me—I said, "You hussy, what have you done with my things?"—she began crying—there was my watch, guard, ring, and four sovereigns, and 10 d. on the table—she said she had bought a gown, a bonnet, and shawl with the remainder of the money.

[trialAccount t18440610-1792]
1792. WILLIAM SMITH was indicted for stealing 11b. weight of jam, value 1 s. 3 d.; 1 jar, 3 d.; 11oz. of mixed spice, 2 s.; 1 bottle, 2 d.; 30lbs. weight of sugar, 19 s.; 3 1/2 lbs. weight of currants, 1 s. 6 d.; loz. weight of mace, 6 d.; and 1 1/2 oz. weight of cassia, 2 d.; the goods of Frederick Hills, his master.
FREDERICK HILLS , confectioner, Kennington. The prisoner was my porter—I missed these things—I went with the officer to the prisoner's house, and found them—the prisoner had no business with them.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST, Q. Did you ask him to show you his lodging? A. We did—we spoke to him first in the bakehouse, and he said he would show us his lodging—I can swear to the jam; I made it—the prisoner said it was mine on the evening we took him, and he said the other articles were not mine—he did not say why he had taken the jam—I found it on his shelf in the room his mother occupied—I found all these articles in that room—the jam is not very stale—he did not deal with me before he came to live with me—I said before the Magistrate that he said the jam was mine—what I said was taken down in writing—I do not find here that he said the jam was mine—( looking at his depositions)—it is an oversight—he did not say the pot was mine—I took up the jam-pot and said, "This is mine"—at that time he said it was mine—he did not say so as we were going to the station—he did not say that the pot was mine and the jam was his, of his own making—I am not aware that he had been in the habit of selling buns—he had been in my employ before.
HENRY LUPTON ( police-constable L 7.) I went with the prosecutor and found these things on a shelf—the sugar was under a pot, and these bottles and other things were under the bed—the prosecutor said, "These are mine, and this bottle of spice I believe to be mine; I lost it about Christmas"—this sugar, he said, was his; he had lost about half a hundred weight of moist sugar at one time, and there were 22lflbs. found there.
Cross-examined. Q. What did the prisoner say? A. That they were not Mr. Hill's, except the raspberry jam—I swear that he said that—there is not a word about his having said the jam was Mr. Hills's in my deposition—it was
stated before the Magistrate, but was not put down—( deposition read)—"The prisoner said—' the whole of the articles found by me were none of Mr. Hill's.'"
MR. PRENDERGAST called
MARY ALGER . Mrs. Smith moved seven years ago, and this bottle of spice was moved by her—I moved it off the table, and gave it her—it is in a gooseberry bottle.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18440610-1794]
1794. JOHN FRYER was indicted for stealing 1 cash-box, value 16 s.; 1 sovereign, 73 shillings, 33 sixpences, and 19 groats; the property of George Hodges, his master.
GEORGE HODGES . I keep a beer-shop—the prisoner was my waiter—I had a cash-box, about the 7th of June, in the counter in the bar—I missed it—there were books and papers in it, a sovereign, and about 5 l. in shillings, sixpences, and fourpenny pieces—I went to the station, brought an officer, gave all my people in charge to the officer, while I and another person went to look over my premises—and in a lobby of the water-closet, I found the box broken open—the books and papers were left in it, but the money was gone—I called the policeman—he took the box from there, and from what Miller said, I gave the prisoner into custody—I searched further, and found 4 l. 15 s. 10 d. in a little piece of rag under the stairs—I had seen my box safe at a quarter-past four, and missed it at six o'clock—the prisoner was there, and thirteen or fourteen other persons.
JACOB MILLER . At half-past five o'clock on the 7th of June, I went to one of the water-closets, I saw it was dirty—I went to the others to see that they were clean—I then went out, shut the door, and sat down just outside the closet—I saw the prisoner come round and go into the water-closet, and shut the door—I then heard a stamping on the floor, and as if a box or something was rattling, and five or six kicks—the prisoner then came out and passed by where I was—he said, "Halloo"—I said, "I am having a pitch "—he passed by, and came again in about five minutes; he said, "I have got a slice of luck "—I said, "What is it?"—he said, "I have found 6 d., and we will go and have a glass of gin"—we went and had it—I came back and
went to take checks at the gate—while I was there, Mr. Hodges came and said, "Come with me"—we were all called in, and the policeman came in—Mr. Hodges said, "I have lost my cash-box"—I said to the prisoner, " Jack you are the man, I heard you breaking the box open"—no one could have gone into that water-closet without my seeing it—the box was found in that water-closet.
Prisoner. When you saw me go in, were you not smoking a pipe? Witness. No, I was leaning against the water-closet—you might have had the box under your coat, I could only see your head—I never knew a stain on your character before.
SAMUEL HOCKADAY ( police-constable L 178.) I found this cash-box in the lobby of the water-closet—I found the cash in this cloth under the stairs.
JACOB MILLER re-examined. I had been sitting down with the prisoner in the fore part of the week, and he opened this napkin, which had his dinner wrapped in it—I noticed it as being a napkin, and this piece, that the money was wrapped in is part of the same napkin.
Prisoner. There were twenty persons on the premises, and they had equal opportunity of getting to the bar as myself.
GUILTY. Aged 26.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18440610-1795]
1795. THOMAS COLLINS was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of Robert Langley.
ROBERT LANGLEY . I am a labourer, and live in Garrett-lane, Wandsworth. [trialAccount t18440610-1797]
1797. ELEANOR STEPHENS was indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 10 s., the goods of Henry Gower.
HENRY GOWER . I live at Barnes. The prisoner lodged in my house—I
lost this shawl—the prisoner came to me—I charged her with having stolen it—she denied it—I said it was no use to deny it, for I had found out where it was pledged in her name—I had been to Putney and seen it—I did not say it would be better for her to tell.
MARY ANN GOWER . This is my husband's shawl—I saw it safe at home on the 20th of May.
GEORGE SPENCER . I am a pawnbroker. I produce this shawl, which I took in of Stowell—she was alone when she pledged it—the bill against her was thrown out—this is the duplicate I gave of it.
JOHN CANNON ( police-constable V 201.) I produce this duplicate, which I found at Stowell's house—I took the prisoner for taking the shawl—she asked me to allow her to dress, which I did—I sat down in the room—she said, "In taking this shawl I have added more to my misfortune."
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is this your writing to this deposition? A. Yes; I was asked if it was correct, and I said it was—( read— " It only added to her misfortune in having anything to do with the shawl")—she said it added to her misfortune in taking the shawl.
HENRY GOWER re-examined. Q. Did you in any way tell her that it would be better for her, or for her advantage, if she would tell you the truth and confess? A. No.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you take your oath you did not say it would be better she should tell you who took it, as you had found out where it was pledged, and in her name? A. Some of those words I did not—I said it was no use to deny it, I had found out where it was pledged.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440610-1798]
1798. JOSEPH CANNON and DANIEL GARRARD were indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
CHRISTOPHER JOHN HILDYARD , draper, Kennington-cross. Between twelve and one o'clock on the 23rd of May, Cannon came into my shop, and asked if I could give him change for a sovereign—I said "Yes"—I tendered him 10 s. in silver, and a half-sovereign—Garrard then came in, and Cannon said to him, "Here is the money I owe you, John," offerring him the half-sovereign—he said, "I would rather have silver," then Cannon asked if I could give them silver—I said, not unless he took all sixpences, which he did chiefly, and I received a counterfeit half-sovereign from one of the prisoners—I could not say which, because the money was laid on the counter—it is our custom to weigh gold—I could not speak to having weighed the half-sovereign I gave them myself—I put the half sovereign they gave me into my pocket, and passed it away to my sister—I saw her give it to the policeman.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you know the prisoners at all? A. No, I never saw them to my knowledge—they were not in my shop many minutes—I know them—they were dressed precisely the same when I saw them again, except Cannon not having the pins in his stock, and a silver-headed cane.
MR. SCRIVEN. Q. When did the officer come into your shop? A. In a minute or two—I have not the slightest doubt that the prisoners are the persons.
ANGELINA FLETCHER HILDYARD . I am sister of Christopher Hildyard. I received a half sovereign from my brother on the 23rd of May—I put it in my purse—there was nothing else there but a few shillings—I did not keep it there very long—I brought my purse down, and gave the half-sovereign to the policeman.
CHARLES GREAVES , assistant to Mr. Dixon, linendraper, Blackfriars-road. On the 22nd of May, Cannon came for change for a sovereign—Garrard stood just outside the door—I gave Cannon four half-crowns, the rest in shillings and sixpences—he said he would rather have a half-sovereign—I gave him one, and took away the four half-crowns—I took the half-sovereign from the desk—there was no other in the place where I took it from—I had weighed it, and it was full weight—I had examined it to see whether it was a good one—after I had handed the half-sovereign to Cannon, he called in Garrard, and said he believed he owed him a half-sovereign—Garrard said he would rather have silver, and Garrard handed the half-sovereign to Cannon—he passed it on to the desk again, and said he would rather have silver—I took the half-sovereign from him, and gave him silver—I believed it to be the same I gave him—I put it back to the desk without examining it—it was done in an instant—it was put back into the bowl where the gold was—there was no other half-sovereign there—they then left the shop—soon after the policeman came—I gave the half-sovereign to Morris—he marked it, and handed it to Goff.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you a distinct recollection of weighing this half-sovereign? A. I received it from Morris, and weighed it—it was the only one I had received that morning—I weighed it by a pair of scales and a stamped weight—I did not examine the bowl at the time I put it in, but I did a few minutes before, when I turned over the gold, there were but two or three sovereigns in the bowl.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Do you know when this half-sovereign was taken? A. About twelve o'clock—there were no others put in the bowl.
WILLIAM MORRIS . I am assistant to Mr. Dixon, in Blackfriars-road. I received a half-sovereign from Mr. Greaves, and marked it—I handed the same to Gough.
Cross-examined. Q. What mark did you put on it? A. A cross on the neck, with a pair of scissors.
BENJAMIN LOCK WOOD . I am assistant to Mr. Bayfield, a pawnbroker, in the New-cut. In coming from Kennington-cross to the Blackfriars-road you can come through the New-cut—on the 22nd of May, Garrard came to me for a sovereign for a pound's worth of silver—I did not see any on else with him.
GEORGE LANGRIDGE , assistant to a pawnbroker, Waterloo-road. On the 22nd of May, Cannon came and asked for a sovereign for a pound's worth of silver—I gave him a new sovereign—he asked me to give him one of that reign.
ESTHER CLARK . On the 22nd of May, I was in the service of a staymaker, in Westminster-road. Cannon came and asked for change for a sovereign, and handed me a new sovereign—I had not got it, and I sent Sarah Jenkins out—she returned with four half-crowns and a half-sovereign change, and handed it to Cannon—he said, "Thank you," and went to the door, and said, "Here, John, is the half-sovereign I owe yon;" and then I saw Garrard—he said, "I should like silver;" and Cannon said, "Perhaps this young person will give you change for the half-sovereign"—I looked for some silver, and gave it to Cannon—he passed me over a half-sovereign—I put it into the till—there was no other half-sovereign there—I am sure of that—they thanked me, and went out—the policeman came in immediately after—I then gave him the half-sovereign I had received from Cannon.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen either of the prisoners before? A. No—I had examined the till before I put the half-sovereign in—Mr. Cox took the cash away the night before, and we had not received a half-sovereign in the course of that day.
SARAH JENKINS . I was sent for change for a sovereign by Clark—I went to a pawnbroker's, and gave the change to Cannon.
WILLIAM THOMAS WEELANDS , pawnbroker, Lambeth. On the 22nd of May, Jenkins came to my shop for change for a sovereign—she asked me to give her a half-sovereign, and the rest in silver—it was a good half-sovereign—we always sound our money on the counter.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you speak to any distinct recollection of having sounded this one, or only your general practice? A. From a recollection of having sounded this one, I know, because Jenkins came in about half an hour after, and asked if I had given her a good half-sovereign—a bad half-sovereign will ring, but not like a good one.
JAMES BROOK ( police-constable L 118.) I was with Goff in the Lambeth-road, between twelve and one o'clock, on the 22nd of May—I saw the two prisoners in conversation together, and followed them to see where they went to—Cannon went into a tobacconist's shop of the name of Compton—they joined company again, and went to Mr. Hildyard's—I remained outside watching—after they came from there I followed them again—I went into Mr. Hill's, came out, and pursued them again—I next saw them in the Kennington-road—they both went into the Duke of Clarence, near the Obelisk, remained there ten minutes or a quarter of an hour, then came out together, and went up the Blackfriars-road, and both went into Mr. Dixon's—Goff went into Dixon's, and I went after the prisoners—they both went into Bayfield's shop—after that they went into a beer-shop in the Waterloo-road, and staid there a short time—after that Cannon went into a pawnbroker's in the Waterloo-road, and then proceeded to the Dover Castle wine-vaults, and stayed there a little while, then into an oil-shop, and then to Cox's—after they came out I went into Mr. Cox—I followed them, and took them both—on searching them I found four half-crowns on each—I received this half-sovereign from Esther Clark, and this one from Miss Hildyard.
Cross-examined. Q. Where have you kept the half-sovereigns? A. Locked up in my desk at home—I have always kept the key—I do not know that I was in a public-house with any of the witnesses just before the examination—I was not tossing for beer with any of them—I do not recollect having beer with any of them—I did not say, "Here, drink, for we shall be better up to our mark"—I recollect being with some of them after the examination—I was drinking with Mr. Morris and Mr. Weelands, at the Catherine Wheel after the examination—I swear it was after that.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-constable L 83.) I produce a half-sovereign, which I got from Morris—I was with Brook on the 22nd of May—I saw the two prisoners in conversation together—I was with Brook when he followed them.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you at a public-house drinking with any of the witnesses on the day of the examination? A. I think I was—I think it was after the examination—I am not sure—I did not hear anything said about drink, and we shall be better up to our mark—there was no tossing to my knowledge.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. These three half-sovereigns are all counterfeit, and all cast in one mould—they are of white metal, and have been gilt to represent a half-sovereign, by a new process of voltaic electricity—they will not ring like genuine half-sovereigns—two of them are very much lighter than a genuine half-sovereign—they will be easily detected, by trying them on the finger, without the assistance of scales.
CANNON — GUILTY . Aged 23.
GARRARD — GUILTY . Aged 16.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18440610-1799]
1799. JOHN RUSSELL , was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES TAYLOR . I keep a shop. On the 4th of June, the prisoner came to my shop for half a pint of porter—he gave me a good half-crown, and I gave him 2 s. 6 d. change—I observed a cross on the head of each shilling—he took them off the counter, and the halfpence also, and said, "Master, you have given me a dark one," and gave me back 1 s., which I saw was a bad one and had no cross on it—I took it and beckoned to a person to stop him—I gave the shilling to West, the officer.
THOMAS WEST ( police-constable M 249.) I received this shilling from Taylor.
MR. JOHN FIELD . This is a counterfeit shilling.
Prisoner's Defence. He said he could not give me change, and then he found the shillings; as soon as he put them down I said this one looks rather dark; he took it up and went into the tap-room, and out came three men, who said it was a good one.
JAMES TAYLOR re-examined. About five minutes elapsed from the time of his passing the bad shilling and his being searched—there were two more behind him, near enough to touch him—I marked the shillings, because it was the fourth time I had been served so.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18440610-1800]
1800. ALEXANDER REVELL was indicted for stealing 1 watch-guard, value 1 s.; 1 cross, 1 s.; 2 keys, 6 d.; 1 reticule, 1 s.; 2 half-crowns, 2 shillings, 2 sixpences, 3 pence, and 4 halfpence, the property of Sarah Shirley; from her person.
SARAH SHIRLEY . I am servant to Mr. Maldon, in the City-road. I was returning down the Kent-road, about ten o'clock at night, on the 28th of May—there were three boys together—one of them made up to me, and took my reticule from my arm—it contained the property stated—this is it—I I do not know who the person was—he ran down a street.
JOSEPH SKINNER . I live at Peckham. I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and saw the prisoner running, and saw him throw this bag down—I took it up with my right hand, and took him with my left—this is the bag.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What are you? A. An auctioneer and appraiser—I have no doubt about the prisoner.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18440610-1801]
1801. GEORGE WESTCOTT was indicted for embezzlement.
CATHARINE SOAMES . I am a widow, and live in Dorset-street, Clapham-road. On the 5th of May I paid the prisoner 7 s. 7 d. for his master, Mr. Fox, for beer, and on the 7th of June 11 s. 1 d.
MARY ANN ROWCLIFFE . I paid the prisoner 2 s. 3 d. on the 2nd of June for his master.
CHARLES FOX . The prisoner was my servant—if he received money, it was his duty to pay it to me directly—if he received 7 s. 7 d. he has not paid it to me at any time—if he received 2 s. 3 d. from Mrs. Rowcliffe he has not paid me, nor 11 s. 1 d. from Soames—he has paid 10 s. on account of what he has had from me.
JOHN MENNIE ( police-constable V 70.) I took the prisoner, and found on him seven bills, which his master had given him to give to customers, but he kept them back, and has eceived the monies weekly from the customers.
Prisoner's Defence. I acknowledge receiving the money; I paid him 10 s. of it, and promised the rest in a week.
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.

NOT GUILTY .
